## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Score is MINIMIZED (lower = better)
- Champion scores -10.98, which means it's delivering good items (decreasing score by 1 each)
- Need to identify which color wall (BLUE or GREEN) corresponds to the "good" item
- The champion uses GoAwayColor (B=9) with clr=2 (green) in states 0 and 2, and GoToColor (B=8) with clr=6 (cyan) in state 1
- Wait - clr values: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: GoAwayColor green (clr=2), cyan LED → goes away from green walls
- State 1: GoToColor cyan (clr=6), magenta LED → goes toward cyan... but cyan isn't a wall color mentioned
- State 2: GoAwayColor green (clr=2), magenta LED → also goes away from green
- State 3: Stop, cyan LED

The champion has -10.98 score, meaning it's delivering good items. The structure seems to navigate toward the nest (red wall) and avoid the green source area, picking up items from the blue source.

**Strategy for Round 2:**
1. Survive: Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit: Make small modifications to champion
3. Explore: Try different topologies

Let me design machines:

1. **Champion (exact copy)** - must include
2. **Exploit 1**: Shift probabilities slightly on champion
3. **Exploit 2**: Adjust transition probabilities in state 1
4. **Exploit 3**: Modify state 2 transitions
5. **Exploit 4**: Try variant with GoToColor red (toward nest) after picking up item
6. **Explore 1**: Simple 2-state: explore → go to red wall (nest)
7. **Explore 2**: 3-state foraging loop: explore source, go to nest, avoid green
8. **Explore 3**: GoToColor blue source, then GoToColor red nest
9. **Explore 4**: 4-state with explicit blue-seeking and red-seeking
10. **Explore 5**: GoAwayColor green + GoToColor red
11. **Explore 6**: Minimal 2-state GoToColor red after black floor
12. **Explore 7**: 3-state with floor detection
13. **Explore 8**: Variant of champion with different color targets

# ROUND 3/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insights from Round 2:**
- The champion is a simple 2-state machine: State 0 = GoAwayColor green (clr=2), transitions to itself on black floor (p=0.9) or seeing yellow (clr=4, p=0.06); State 1 = GoToColor red (clr=1), transitions to State 0 on white floor (p=0.85) or gray floor (p=0.4)
- This makes sense: avoid green (bad source), go toward red wall (nest) after picking up item from blue source (black floor)
- The robot: enters black floor (blue source) → picks up good item → goes toward red wall (nest) → delivers → goes away from green
- Score -11.36 means ~11-12 items delivered per run

**What the 2-state champion does:**
- State 0 (GoAwayGreen): on black floor → stay in state 0 (keep collecting from blue source); sees yellow LED → stay in state 0
- State 1 (GoToRed): on white floor → go to state 0; on gray → go to state 0

Wait, both transitions in state 1 go to state 0 (D=0 means the other state). With 2 states, leaving state 0, D=0 means state 1... no wait: "leaving state 0, the others are [1]" so D=0 = state 1. And "leaving state 1, the others are [0]" so D=0 = state 0.

So:
- State 0 (GoAwayGreen): black floor → go to state 1 (GoToRed); sees yellow → go to state 1
- State 1 (GoToRed): white floor → go to state 0 (GoAwayGreen); gray floor → go to state 0

This makes perfect sense! Robot goes away from green, enters blue source (black floor), then goes toward red wall (nest), delivers on white floor, then goes away from green again.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit: try small parameter tweaks on the 2-state champion
3. Explore: add a 3rd state for more nuanced behavior, try different probabilities

## Reasoning

**Key insights from Round 3:**
- Champion is now -11.72, a 4-state machine
- The 4-state champion structure:
  - State 0: GoAwayGreen (clr=2), transitions to state 1 on black floor (p=0.9) or seeing yellow (p=0.06)
  - State 1: GoToRed (clr=1), transitions to state 0 on white floor (p=0.85) or gray floor (p=0.4)
  - State 2: GoAwayGreen (clr=2), transitions to state 1 on black floor (p=0.9) or white floor (p=0.5)
  - State 3: Stop, transitions to state 2 on white floor (p=0.6) or black floor (p=0.9)
- State 3 (Stop) seems like a delivery pause - when on white (nest) stop briefly, then go to state 2 (GoAwayGreen)
- The extra states seem to help coordination

**Analysis of what works:**
- GoAwayColor green (clr=2) is key - pushes robots toward blue source
- GoToColor red (clr=1) navigates back to nest
- Black floor detection triggers transition to "go to nest" phase
- White floor detection triggers return to foraging

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Tune probabilities on champion (±10%)
- Try adding a transition to state 1 (GoToRed) that also checks for seeing yellow LEDs
- Modify state 3 transitions

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 4:**
- Champion stays at -11.72 (4-state machine)
- The best performers this round cluster around -11.1 to -11.4, all variants of the champion
- The champion topology is stable: GoAwayGreen → GoToRed → GoAwayGreen(again) → Stop
- Small parameter variations haven't beaten the champion
- Worst performers: adding white-floor transition to state 0, using exploration in state 2, adding transitions in state 0

**What I notice:**
- The champion's state 3 (Stop) seems to act as a delivery pause: when robot is on white floor (nest), it stops briefly, then goes to state 2 (GoAwayGreen) or stays stopped on black floor
- State 2 (GoAwayGreen) transitions to state 1 (GoToRed) on black floor - this is the pickup trigger
- The loop: S0(GoAwayGreen) → black floor → S1(GoToRed) → white floor → S0 or gray → S0
- S2 is essentially a duplicate of S0 with slightly different parameters
- S3 Stop is reached from S1 on white floor transition

**Strategy for Round 5:**
1. Survive: keep champion
2. Exploit: fine-tune champion parameters more carefully, especially the stop state transitions
3. Try: adding a 3rd transition to some states with different condition types
4. Explore: try varying the stop state behavior, try GoToColor blue (clr=3) instead of GoAwayGreen

Key observations:
- Adding white-floor transition to state 0 hurts (worst performer #3 used it with extra transition)
- Using exploration in state 2 hurts
- The champion's structure is near-optimal; need finer parameter search or topology variation

Let me try:
- Varying p values around champion more carefully
- Adding neighbor-based conditions
- Trying state 3 as GoToRed instead of Stop (to keep moving toward nest)
- Trying different LED colors to affect perceived-color transitions

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5:**
- Champion unchanged at -11.72 from Round 3
- All Round 5 performers scored between -10.76 and -10.93, WORSE than champion
- We're stuck in a local optimum - fine-tuning parameters isn't helping
- The champion's exact parameters seem hard to beat with small perturbations
- Notable: the champion itself appeared in worst performers this round (score -10.76 in one run), showing high variance

**Key observations:**
- The champion's topology: S0(GoAwayGreen)→S1(GoToRed)→S0/S3; S2(GoAwayGreen)→S1; S3(Stop)→S0/S2
- This creates a foraging loop: avoid green → black floor detected → go to red wall → white floor → stop → go away from green again
- The "stop on white" (state 3) seems important - allows delivery to complete
- Changing state 3 to GoToRed (B=8 clr=1) made things worse (-10.80)

**New exploration directions:**
1. Try completely different topologies - maybe 3-state without the stop
2. Try higher-level exploration: GoToColor blue (clr=3) to seek the source directly
3. Try adding a magenta LED detection (clr=5) since state 1 uses magenta LED - robots could signal delivery
4. Try varying the LED colors to create communication signals
5. Try GoAwayColor with different colors (clr=3 blue instead of clr=2 green)
6. Try adding floor-color transitions to the stop state (state 3)
7. Try a 4-state machine where state 2 is GoToColor blue (source seeking) and state 3 handles the white floor

The champion uses: GoAwayGreen to push toward blue source. The key insight is robots avoid green and naturally find blue. Let me try machines that explicitly seek blue (clr=3) as well.

Also: Let me try machines with different state 3 behaviors - perhaps GoToColor red (to actively seek nest) instead of Stop, but with the right transitions.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 6:**
- Champion remains at -11.72, unchanged for 4 rounds
- Best performers this round score around -10.85 to -11.00, all worse than champion
- The champion appears to have high variance (same machine scored -10.85 in round 6 vs -11.72 when it was discovered)
- We're clearly stuck - the champion topology is being explored exhaustively with diminishing returns

**Critical observations:**
- GoToColor blue (clr=3) as state 0 was worst performer (score 5.60) - confirms blue wall seeking is wrong
- Using yellow LED (cle1=4) in state 1 caused interference with the yellow-detection transition in state 0 (score -7.53)
- The 3-state stop variant with white-floor transition to stop state was -2.02 (bad)
- The champion's exact parameters keep winning but we can't beat them

**What might actually improve:**
1. The champion has high variance - running it multiple times shows -10.85 to -11.72. Maybe we need fundamentally different approaches.
2. Try: explicit two-phase foraging - seek source (GoToColor blue/black detection) then explicit nest return
3. Try: using magenta LED detection (l=5) since state 1 uses magenta LED - robots could follow each other to nest
4. Try: 4-state with an exploration state for when robots are lost
5. Try: modifying the transition probability in state 0 from black floor - currently 0.9, maybe lower (0.7) keeps robots in source longer
6. Try: state 3 with gray floor detection (robots in transit)

**New ideas:**
- The champion transitions on "sees yellow LED" (l=4) in state 0 - but state 3 uses magenta (cle3=5), not yellow. So who has yellow LEDs? State 0 and 2 use cyan (cle=6). No state has yellow LEDs! So the yellow-detection transition (l0x1=4) likely never fires, making state 0 effectively a 1-transition state. This means the yellow detection is essentially dead code.
- What if we use magenta detection (l=5) since state 1 actually emits magenta? Robots in state 1 (GoToRed) emit magenta - detecting them could signal "going to nest, follow".

Let me try machines with:
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Replace yellow detection with magenta detection in state 0
3. Remove the dead yellow-detection transition
4. Try state 3 gray-floor transition instead of black-floor
5. Cross between champion and 2-state champion (from round 2)
6. Novel topologies with different LED/detection schemes

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**
- Champion still at -11.72, unchanged for 5 rounds
- Best performers this round: -10.37 to -10.74, all worse than champion
- The 2-state machine (champion from round 2) scored -10.74 - still competitive
- Variants are consistently scoring ~1 point worse than the champion
- The champion has high run-to-run variance - it's a stochastic system

**Critical realization:**
The champion's score of -11.72 may itself be a lucky run. The same machine scores -10.35 to -10.85 in other rounds. The true mean might be around -11. We're essentially exploring variants of a near-optimal solution.

**What I haven't tried much:**
1. Crossbreeding the 2-state and 4-state champions
2. Trying different transition conditions in state 2 (currently black→state1, gray→state1)
3. The state 2 "gray floor → state 1" transition: when robot is on gray (transit), go to state 1 (GoToRed). This seems backwards - gray means in transit, not at source. Maybe state 2 should only trigger on black floor.
4. State 3 (Stop) has black-floor transition (p=0.9) - why would a stopped robot on black floor go back to state 2? This might cause robots to stop at the source.

**State 3 analysis:** 
- Transition: white floor (p=0.6) → state 2; black floor (p=0.9) → state 0
- So on white: go to state 2 (GoAwayGreen); on black: go to state 0 (GoAwayGreen)
- Both transitions lead to GoAwayGreen states - this makes sense as a reset after delivery

**New ideas:**
- Try state 3 with only one transition (white floor only)
- Try state 2 with only black floor trigger (remove gray trigger)
- Try replacing gray trigger in state 2 with white trigger
- Try a 3-state machine where state 2 is Stop (simpler than 4-state)
- Try adding a gray-floor transition to state 1 (GoToRed) to handle transit better

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8:**
- Champion still unchanged at -11.72 (6 rounds)
- Best performer was -10.89, consistently worse
- The champion appears to be a lucky high-variance run
- Key findings: 
  - Adding black-floor transition to state 2 (keeping robots in source longer) helped slightly (-10.89)
  - Simplifying state 2 to 1 transition (black floor only) scored -10.54
  - Adding white-floor transition to state 1 that leads to state 3 (stop) scored -10.54
  - Adding black-floor transition to state 1 leading to state 2 is VERY BAD (-6.90)

**Strategic shift for Round 9:**
We have 2 rounds left. The champion is unlikely to be beaten with minor tweaks. I need genuinely different structures. Let me think about what the champion really does:

- S0 (GoAwayGreen, cyan LED): black floor → S1; sees yellow → S1
- S1 (GoToRed, magenta LED): white floor → S0; gray floor → S0  
- S2 (GoAwayGreen, cyan LED): black floor → S1; gray floor → S1
- S3 (Stop, magenta LED): white floor → S2; black floor → S0

The flow: robots start, go away from green (find blue source), step on black → go to red wall (nest), arrive at white → go away from green again (or stop briefly). State 3 (Stop) is reached when state 1 transitions on white floor... wait, state 1 goes to state 0 on white, and state 0 goes to state 1 on black. So how does state 3 get reached?

State 3 destination indices: with 4 states, from state 1 leaving, others are [0,2,3]. So n1x0=0 targets state 0, n1x1=0 also targets state 0... wait, both transitions go to D=0 which is state 0. State 3 is only reachable from state 2 (n2x1 targets state 1 = D=1 which maps to... with 4 states, from state 2, others are [0,1,3], so D=0=state0, D=1=state1, D=2=state3). So n2x1 (D=1) → state 1, and n2x0 (D=0)... wait let me re-read.

"With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2." So for 4 states, leaving state 2, others are [0,1,3]: D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→state3.

State 2: n2x0 D=1 → state 1; n2x1 D=1 → state 1. Both go to state 1!
State 3: n3x0 D=2 → ? From state 3, others are [0,1,2]: D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→state2. So n3x0 D=2 → state2; n3x1 D=0 → state0.

So state 3 is reachable from... nobody goes to D=2 from state 1 or state 0. State 3 is UNREACHABLE in the champion! It's dead code. The champion is effectively a 3-state machine (S0, S1, S2) with S3 never visited.

This changes everything. The effective champion is:
- S0 (GoAwayGreen): black→S1; sees yellow→S1
- S1 (GoToRed): white→S0; gray→S0
- S2 (GoAwayGreen): black→S1; gray→S1

And S2 is reachable from... S0 goes to S1 (D=0 from S0, others=[1,2,3], D=0→state1). S1 goes to S0 (D=0, others=[0,2,3], D=0→state0). Nobody reaches S2 either! The champion may effectively be just S0 and S1!

Actually wait: from state 0, others=[1,2,3] (all states except 0), so D=0→state1, D=1→state2, D=2→state3. So n0x0=1 means D=1→state2... no wait n0x0 is the destination index. Let me re-read: "--n0x0 1" means D=1 which targets state 2 (second in [1,2,3]).

Actually the champion: `--n0x0 1 --c0x0 0` means from state 0, transition 0 has destination D=1. From state 0, others are [1,2,3], so D=1 → state 2. And `--n0x1 1 --c0x1 7` means destination D=1 → state 2 also. So both transitions from S0 go to S2!

And from S1: `--n1x0 0 --c1x0 2` D=0 → state 0; `--n1x1 0 --c1x1 1` D=0 → state 0.
From S2: `--n2x0 1 --c2x0 0` D=1 from state 2, others=[0,1,3], D=1→state1; `--n2x1 1 --c2x1 2` D=1→state1.
From S3: unreachable.

So the actual flow is: S0→S2→S1→S0→S2→... S3 is dead.
- S0 (GoAwayGreen): any black floor or yellow sighting → S2
- S2 (GoAwayGreen): black floor → S1; gray floor → S1  
- S1 (GoToRed): white floor → S0; gray floor → S0

This is: avoid green → arrive at source (black) → go to red wall → arrive at white/gray → avoid green again. Makes sense! S0 and S2 both do GoAwayGreen but S2 transitions more eagerly to GoToRed.

**Insight:** The two GoAwayGreen states create different dwell times. S0 only transitions on black (or rare yellow), while S2 transitions on BOTH black AND gray - so S2 exits toward nest sooner. This means after leaving nest (white/gray → S0), robots spend time in S0 (more persistent GoAwayGreen) before finding source.

**New exploration ideas:**
1. Try making S0 transition on gray too (more aggressive)
2. Try different transition probabilities more carefully
3. Try making S2 transition only on black (not gray)
4. Try 3-state version that captures this insight
5. Try adding a 4th active state for something useful

## Reasoning

**Final round analysis:**
- Champion still at -11.72 (unchanged for 7 rounds)
- Best performer this round: -11.62 (very close!) - the variant with an extra black-floor self-loop in state 2
- Second best: -11.32 - variant with gray-floor detection added to state 0
- The score -11.62 is the closest we've gotten to the champion in many rounds

**Key new findings from Round 9:**
1. **Best performer** (-11.62): Added `--n2x2 2 --c2x2 0 --p2x2 0.4` to state 2 - a black-floor transition that stays in state 2 (D=2 from state 2, others=[0,1,3], so D=2→state3... wait, state 3 is unreachable normally). Actually D=2 from state 2 → state 3 (the stop state). This means on black floor with p=0.4, go to stop state! That's interesting - robots sometimes stop at source before going to nest.
2. **Second best** (-11.32): Added gray-floor detection to state 0 with p=0.3, triggering transition to state 2. This makes state 0 more eager to transition.
3. **5th best** (-11.05): Replaced yellow-LED detection in state 0 with gray-floor detection (p=0.4) - also works well.

**Strategy for final round:**
- Combine the best features: state 2 with extra black-floor transition AND state 0 with gray-floor trigger
- Try varying the p values of the best-performing variant (-11.62)
- Try the best performer with additional tweaks
- Keep champion
- A few exploratory combinations of the top variants

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 15 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.38 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
